========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s changed. I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything at all, because I can still sort of function and have normal conversations at work, and there are moments where something is fine, but most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running underneath everything.

Like, I used to get genuinely excited about baking, even just planning what to make or trying some little adjustment to a recipe, and now it feels more like… I know that’s something I used to like, but I don’t feel that same lift from it. Same with checking the telescope or nerding out about something in the lab — I can still do it, I can still talk about it, but it doesn’t feel light or satisfying the way it used to. It’s more muted, I guess. Like the volume got turned down on things that used to make me feel interested or happy.

I don’t know that I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a strong word. Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.

I guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.

So, yeah, a little over two years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a strong word. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, definitely, and I’ll think things like, “Why can’t I just handle this better?” or “Why is this so hard when other people seem to manage fine?” But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless.\n\nGuilt… maybe sometimes, but not in a big dramatic way. More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. But I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more… disappointment in myself, I guess, or irritation with myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.\n\nI guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I get tense sometimes, like anyone does, and I can overthink things, but I don’t know if I’m walking around feeling anxious or on edge most of the time.

It’s more like… I feel kind of weighed down or slowed down than keyed up, if that makes sense. I’m not usually jumpy or panicky or constantly expecting something bad to happen. There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.

But I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.

So… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.

More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. But I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more… disappointment in myself, I guess, or irritation with myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.\n\nI guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I get tense sometimes, like anyone does, and I can overthink things, but I don’t know if I’m walking around feeling anxious or on edge most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel kind of weighed down or slowed down than keyed up, if that makes sense. I’m not usually jumpy or panicky or constantly expecting something bad to happen. There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.

I don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.

So yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.

I mean, I get frustrated with myself, definitely, and I’ll think things like, “Why can’t I just handle this better?” or “Why is this so hard when other people seem to manage fine?” But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless.\n\nGuilt… maybe sometimes, but not in a big dramatic way. More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.

Like, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.

So I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.

Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.

It feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.

So I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.

There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.\n\nI don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just feel... worn down all the time. Like, not just “I had a busy week” tired, but this kind of constant low battery feeling. Even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I’m still dragging, and then I have to get through work and whatever needs doing at home, and by the time I’ve dealt with all of that—especially just the house clutter and everyday stuff—I feel like there’s almost nothing left in me for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything at all, because I can still sort of function and have normal conversations at work, and there are moments where something is fine, but most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running underneath everything.\n\nLike, I used to get genuinely excited about baking, even just planning what to make or trying some little adjustment to a recipe, and now it feels more like… I know that’s something I used to like, but I don’t feel that same lift from it. Same with checking the telescope or nerding out about something in the lab — I can still do it, I can still talk about it, but it doesn’t feel light or satisfying the way it used to. It’s more muted, I guess. Like the volume got turned down on things that used to make me feel interested or happy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it didn’t feel like one day I woke up and everything changed. It was more like this low baseline slowly became the normal background.\n\nBut if I trace it back, it started not long after we moved into the older house. At first I thought it was just the move, or the adjustment, or being tired from all the stuff that comes with an older place. But it just kind of… stayed. So, yeah, a little over two years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a strong word. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, definitely, and I’ll think things like, “Why can’t I just handle this better?” or “Why is this so hard when other people seem to manage fine?” But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless.\n\nGuilt… maybe sometimes, but not in a big dramatic way. More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. But I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more… disappointment in myself, I guess, or irritation with myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.\n\nI guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I get tense sometimes, like anyone does, and I can overthink things, but I don’t know if I’m walking around feeling anxious or on edge most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel kind of weighed down or slowed down than keyed up, if that makes sense. I’m not usually jumpy or panicky or constantly expecting something bad to happen. There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.\n\nI don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt consistently better, without these low feelings, since they first started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. But I can’t think of a full two months where I felt consistently back to normal, without that low feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s more like… since shortly after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago — my baseline just shifted downward. Some days it’s quieter, and I can almost convince myself it’s not there, but when I really think about it, it hasn’t fully gone away for that long. It’s just been sort of steadily there, in different intensities.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt consistently better, without these low feelings, since they first started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. But I can’t think of a full two months where I felt consistently back to normal, without that low feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s more like… since shortly after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago — my baseline just shifted downward. Some days it’s quieter, and I can almost convince myself it’s not there, but when I really think about it, it hasn’t fully gone away for that long. It’s just been sort of steadily there, in different intensities.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt this low, gray mood for most of every day, nearly every day, since it started over two years ago?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 39 / physical_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days there’s this low, gray mood running in the background, like I can still go to the lab and talk to people, but I don’t really feel light or excited about things like baking or checking the telescope the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… most days, yes. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, because I can get absorbed in work or a conversation and not be actively thinking, “I feel low right now.” But if I step back and look at the whole day, it’s usually there in the background.

It’s like this dull, gray layer over things. I can still go to the lab, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, answer emails, whatever. I’m functioning. But I don’t feel light, if that makes sense. Things that used to give me a little lift — baking something, checking on the telescope, getting curious about some small thing — they don’t land the same way. I’ll do them, and part of me knows I used to enjoy them more, but the feeling is kind of muted.

So, nearly every day, yes. It’s not always intense, and I can sometimes push it to the side, but it’s there most of the time.

I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything at all, because I can still sort of function and have normal conversations at work, and there are moments where something is fine, but most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running underneath everything.\n\nLike, I used to get genuinely excited about baking, even just planning what to make or trying some little adjustment to a recipe, and now it feels more like… I know that’s something I used to like, but I don’t feel that same lift from it. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. I can still go to the lab, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, answer emails, whatever. I’m functioning. But I don’t feel light, if that makes sense. Things that used to give me a little lift — baking something, checking on the telescope, getting curious about some small thing — they don’t land the same way. I’ll do them, and part of me knows I used to enjoy them more, but the feeling is kind of muted.\n\nSo, nearly every day, yes. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just feel... worn down all the time. Like, not just “I had a busy week” tired, but this kind of constant low battery feeling. Even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I’m still dragging, and then I have to get through work and whatever needs doing at home, and by the time I’ve dealt with all of that—especially just the house clutter and everyday stuff—I feel like there’s almost nothing left in me for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything at all, because I can still sort of function and have normal conversations at work, and there are moments where something is fine, but most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running underneath everything.\n\nLike, I used to get genuinely excited about baking, even just planning what to make or trying some little adjustment to a recipe, and now it feels more like… I know that’s something I used to like, but I don’t feel that same lift from it. Same with checking the telescope or nerding out about something in the lab — I can still do it, I can still talk about it, but it doesn’t feel light or satisfying the way it used to. It’s more muted, I guess. Like the volume got turned down on things that used to make me feel interested or happy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it didn’t feel like one day I woke up and everything changed. It was more like this low baseline slowly became the normal background.\n\nBut if I trace it back, it started not long after we moved into the older house. At first I thought it was just the move, or the adjustment, or being tired from all the stuff that comes with an older place. But it just kind of… stayed. So, yeah, a little over two years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a strong word. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, definitely, and I’ll think things like, “Why can’t I just handle this better?” or “Why is this so hard when other people seem to manage fine?” But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless.\n\nGuilt… maybe sometimes, but not in a big dramatic way. More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. But I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more… disappointment in myself, I guess, or irritation with myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.\n\nI guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I get tense sometimes, like anyone does, and I can overthink things, but I don’t know if I’m walking around feeling anxious or on edge most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel kind of weighed down or slowed down than keyed up, if that makes sense. I’m not usually jumpy or panicky or constantly expecting something bad to happen. There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.\n\nI don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.\n\nI don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt consistently better, without these low feelings, since they first started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. But I can’t think of a full two months where I felt consistently back to normal, without that low feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s more like… since shortly after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago — my baseline just shifted downward. Some days it’s quieter, and I can almost convince myself it’s not there, but when I really think about it, it hasn’t fully gone away for that long. It’s just been sort of steadily there, in different intensities.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low, gray mood for most of every day, nearly every day, since it started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, yes. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, because I can get absorbed in work or a conversation and not be actively thinking, “I feel low right now.” But if I step back and look at the whole day, it’s usually there in the background.\n\nIt’s like this dull, gray layer over things. I can still go to the lab, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, answer emails, whatever. I’m functioning. But I don’t feel light, if that makes sense. Things that used to give me a little lift — baking something, checking on the telescope, getting curious about some small thing — they don’t land the same way. I’ll do them, and part of me knows I used to enjoy them more, but the feeling is kind of muted.\n\nSo, nearly every day, yes. It’s not always intense, and I can sometimes push it to the side, but it’s there most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period since you first started feeling this way, over two years ago, where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think there has been. I keep trying to think if there was some stretch where I felt fully like myself again, because there have been better weeks here and there, but not a few months where it was just gone.\n\nThis lower baseline started shortly after we moved into the older house, a little over two years ago, and it’s kind of just… stayed. It hasn’t been the exact same every day, but I wouldn’t say I ever got completely back to normal for months at a time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, not just “I had a busy week” tired, but this kind of constant low battery feeling. Even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I’m still dragging, and then I have to get through work and whatever needs doing at home, and by the time I’ve dealt with all of that—especially just the house clutter and everyday stuff—I feel like there’s almost nothing left in me for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything at all, because I can still sort of function and have normal conversations at work, and there are moments where something is fine, but most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running underneath everything.\n\nLike, I used to get genuinely excited about baking, even just planning what to make or trying some little adjustment to a recipe, and now it feels more like… I know that’s something I used to like, but I don’t feel that same lift from it. Same with checking the telescope or nerding out about something in the lab — I can still do it, I can still talk about it, but it doesn’t feel light or satisfying the way it used to. It’s more muted, I guess. Like the volume got turned down on things that used to make me feel interested or happy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it didn’t feel like one day I woke up and everything changed. It was more like this low baseline slowly became the normal background.\n\nBut if I trace it back, it started not long after we moved into the older house. At first I thought it was just the move, or the adjustment, or being tired from all the stuff that comes with an older place. But it just kind of… stayed. So, yeah, a little over two years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a strong word. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, definitely, and I’ll think things like, “Why can’t I just handle this better?” or “Why is this so hard when other people seem to manage fine?” But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless.\n\nGuilt… maybe sometimes, but not in a big dramatic way. More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. But I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more… disappointment in myself, I guess, or irritation with myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.\n\nI guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I get tense sometimes, like anyone does, and I can overthink things, but I don’t know if I’m walking around feeling anxious or on edge most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel kind of weighed down or slowed down than keyed up, if that makes sense. I’m not usually jumpy or panicky or constantly expecting something bad to happen. There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.\n\nI don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt consistently better, without these low feelings, since they first started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. But I can’t think of a full two months where I felt consistently back to normal, without that low feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s more like… since shortly after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago — my baseline just shifted downward. Some days it’s quieter, and I can almost convince myself it’s not there, but when I really think about it, it hasn’t fully gone away for that long. It’s just been sort of steadily there, in different intensities.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low, gray mood for most of every day, nearly every day, since it started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, yes. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, because I can get absorbed in work or a conversation and not be actively thinking, “I feel low right now.” But if I step back and look at the whole day, it’s usually there in the background.\n\nIt’s like this dull, gray layer over things. I can still go to the lab, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, answer emails, whatever. I’m functioning. But I don’t feel light, if that makes sense. Things that used to give me a little lift — baking something, checking on the telescope, getting curious about some small thing — they don’t land the same way. I’ll do them, and part of me knows I used to enjoy them more, but the feeling is kind of muted.\n\nSo, nearly every day, yes. It’s not always intense, and I can sometimes push it to the side, but it’s there most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period since you first started feeling this way, over two years ago, where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think there has been. I keep trying to think if there was some stretch where I felt fully like myself again, because there have been better weeks here and there, but not a few months where it was just gone.\n\nThis lower baseline started shortly after we moved into the older house, a little over two years ago, and it’s kind of just… stayed. It hasn’t been the exact same every day, but I wouldn’t say I ever got completely back to normal for months at a time.\n\nDoctor: Has your low mood been consistently present for the entire two years since it started, without any breaks or periods where it significantly improved?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, as far as I can tell, it’s been there the whole time. It started not long after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago now — and since then it’s like my normal level just dropped and never really came back up.\n\nThere have been days where I’m distracted or things feel a little easier, so I don’t want to make it sound like every single day has been exactly the same. But I can’t point to any real break where it significantly lifted and stayed lifted. It’s more like it’s always been sitting underneath everything, sometimes stronger, sometimes quieter, but still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your low mood tends to worsen or improve at specific times of the year?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear seasonal pattern, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that because it seems like the kind of thing that might make sense — like winter being worse or summer being better — but I can’t really point to a specific time of year where it reliably shifts.\n\nThere are days or weeks that feel a little heavier or a little easier, but I haven’t connected that to the seasons in any consistent way. It’s more like this same background low feeling just sort of continues through the year, and other things might make it feel louder or quieter, but I don’t think I’ve seen a predictable yearly cycle.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Not really, no. I mean, it has had little ups and downs, so there have been times where I might have said, “Okay, this week is a little better,” or I was functioning a bit more normally. But I wouldn’t say it significantly improved and then came back later, like a clear break.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just feel... worn down all the time. Like, not just “I had a busy week” tired, but this kind of constant low battery feeling. Even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I’m still dragging, and then I have to get through work and whatever needs doing at home, and by the time I’ve dealt with all of that—especially just the house clutter and everyday stuff—I feel like there’s almost nothing left in me for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything at all, because I can still sort of function and have normal conversations at work, and there are moments where something is fine, but most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running underneath everything.\n\nLike, I used to get genuinely excited about baking, even just planning what to make or trying some little adjustment to a recipe, and now it feels more like… I know that’s something I used to like, but I don’t feel that same lift from it. Same with checking the telescope or nerding out about something in the lab — I can still do it, I can still talk about it, but it doesn’t feel light or satisfying the way it used to. It’s more muted, I guess. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it didn’t feel like one day I woke up and everything changed. It was more like this low baseline slowly became the normal background.\n\nBut if I trace it back, it started not long after we moved into the older house. At first I thought it was just the move, or the adjustment, or being tired from all the stuff that comes with an older place. But it just kind of… stayed. More like I feel bad when I’m not keeping up with things the way I think I should, or when I feel like I’m not being as present or useful as I want to be. But I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more… disappointment in myself, I guess, or irritation with myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying at any point since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of actually wanting to die, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m tired of feeling like this, and I’ll think something like, “I just want a break from my own head,” but it doesn’t turn into picturing myself dead or wishing I wouldn’t wake up or anything like that.\n\nI guess death is one of those things that crosses your mind in an abstract way sometimes, like when you hear about someone getting sick or there’s something in the news, but since this started, I haven’t been sitting around thinking about dying as a solution or making plans or anything. It’s more… I get worn down and discouraged, but not in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I get tense sometimes, like anyone does, and I can overthink things, but I don’t know if I’m walking around feeling anxious or on edge most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel kind of weighed down or slowed down than keyed up, if that makes sense. I’m not usually jumpy or panicky or constantly expecting something bad to happen. There are moments where I’m irritable or kind of braced for things, but I haven’t really thought of anxiety as the main thing. So… no, I don’t think often, unless I’m missing what you mean by “on edge.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my weight sort of goes up and down a little in the normal way, like depending on how busy I am or what I’ve been eating, but I haven’t noticed anything I’d call significant.\n\nI’m not someone who tracks it super closely every day, so I suppose it’s possible there’s been a small change, but my clothes fit about the same, and nobody has commented on it or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major weight change since I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions or has that also become less enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it sort of depends on the person and the situation. I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying people altogether, no. Like, if I’m with someone I’m comfortable with and the conversation is easy, I can still laugh or feel glad I went.\n\nBut I do think sometimes it takes more effort to get myself there, if that makes sense. The actual social interaction can be okay once I’m in it, but before it happens I might think, “Ugh, do I have the energy for this?” And if it’s a bigger group or something where I have to be “on,” that can feel less appealing than it used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say social interactions are completely not pleasurable. They can still be nice. It’s just not always as automatic, maybe, or I don’t look forward to them as much every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute in a dramatic way, but most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like I can still get up and go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem basically fine. But internally it’s like the color’s been turned down a little.\n\nI don’t know if I’d always call it “sad,” exactly. Sometimes it’s more empty, or flat, or just… heavy. I used to get more naturally excited about little things, like baking something on a weekend or checking the telescope and feeling that spark of curiosity. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt consistently better, without these low feelings, since they first started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. But I can’t think of a full two months where I felt consistently back to normal, without that low feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s more like… since shortly after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago — my baseline just shifted downward. Some days it’s quieter, and I can almost convince myself it’s not there, but when I really think about it, it hasn’t fully gone away for that long. It’s just been sort of steadily there, in different intensities.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low, gray mood for most of every day, nearly every day, since it started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, yes. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, because I can get absorbed in work or a conversation and not be actively thinking, “I feel low right now.” But if I step back and look at the whole day, it’s usually there in the background.\n\nIt’s like this dull, gray layer over things. I can still go to the lab, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, answer emails, whatever. I’m functioning. But I don’t feel light, if that makes sense. I’ll do them, and part of me knows I used to enjoy them more, but the feeling is kind of muted.\n\nSo, nearly every day, yes. I can still do those things, but they don’t feel as light or satisfying as they used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions and waiting for that feeling to show up, and it doesn’t really.\n\nSo yes, I’d say most days, for a lot of the day, there’s that low mood there. It’s just not always obvious because I can still function around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, at least somewhat. It’s not like I’ve stopped doing everything I used to enjoy, but most days there’s this low, gray mood kind of running in the background, and it makes the things I used to look forward to feel less… bright, I guess.\n\nLike, I can still go to the lab, I can talk to people, I can get through the day and do what needs doing. And I still technically like some of the same things — baking, for example, or checking on the telescope, or just getting absorbed in something curious or hands-on. But the feeling I used to get from them isn’t as easy to access. I used to feel this little lift from those things, like a genuine spark of interest or comfort, and now it’s more muted.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I have no interest at all. It’s more that I don’t feel light or excited about things the way I used to, and that’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now, only to have these low feelings return?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there have been better days or stretches where I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel as noticeable, but if you mean like a real period where I felt back to my old self for a while and then it came back, I don’t think so, not really.\n\nIt feels more like this lower baseline has just been there for a long time. I started noticing it shortly after we moved into the older house, and that was a little over two years ago now. At first I thought it was just the stress of the move, or settling in, or maybe needing time to adjust, but then it didn’t really lift in the way I expected it to. It kind of became the background setting.\n\nSo I can have a decent week, or a good conversation, or a day where I’m productive and think, “Okay, maybe I’m fine,” but underneath that it still feels like the same lower level is there. I wouldn’t say it disappears for months and then returns. It’s more like it’s been pretty consistently there since around that time, just louder or quieter depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt consistently better, without these low feelings, since they first started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had better days, definitely, and maybe even little stretches where I was busy enough or things were going smoothly enough that I didn’t focus on it as much. But I can’t think of a full two months where I felt consistently back to normal, without that low feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s more like… since shortly after we moved into the older house — a little over two years ago — my baseline just shifted downward. Some days it’s quieter, and I can almost convince myself it’s not there, but when I really think about it, it hasn’t fully gone away for that long. It’s just been sort of steadily there, in different intensities.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low, gray mood for most of every day, nearly every day, since it started over two years ago?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, yes. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, because I can get absorbed in work or a conversation and not be actively thinking, “I feel low right now.” But if I step back and look at the whole day, it’s usually there in the background.\n\nIt’s like this dull, gray layer over things. I can still go to the lab, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, answer emails, whatever. I’m functioning. But I don’t feel light, if that makes sense. Things that used to give me a little lift — baking something, checking on the telescope, getting curious about some small thing — they don’t land the same way. I’ll do them, and part of me knows I used to enjoy them more, but the feeling is kind of muted.\n\nSo, nearly every day, yes. It’s not always intense, and I can sometimes push it to the side, but it’s there most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period since you first started feeling this way, over two years ago, where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think there has been.
